The [[concepts/imagination|imagination]] was as much in [[motifs/prison|prison]] as I was. [[concepts/vanity|Vanity]] had barred up the windows, and the name of the warder was Hate.
All this took place in the early part of November of the year before last. A great [[motifs/river|river]] of life flows between you and a date so distant. Hardly, if at all, can you see across so wide a waste, But to me it seems to have occurred, I will not say yesterday, but today. [[concepts/suffering|Suffering]] is one long moment. We cannot divide it by [[motifs/seasons|seasons]]. We can only record its moods, and chronicle their return. With us [[motifs/time|time]] itself does not progress. It revolves. It seems to circle round one centre of [[motifs/pain|pain]]. The paralysing immobility of a life, every circumstance of which is regulated after an unchangeable pattern, so that we eat and drink and walk and lie down and pray, or kneel at least for [[motifs/prayer|prayer]], according to the inflexible laws of an iron formula: this immobile quality, that makes each dreadful day in the very minutest detail like its brother, seems to communicate itself to those external forces the very essence of whose existence is ceaseless change. Of seed-[[motifs/time|time]] or harvest, of the reapers bending over the corn, or the grape-gatherers threading through the vines, of the grass in the orchard made white with broken blossoms, or strewn with fallen fruit, we know nothing, and can know nothing. For us there is only one season, the season of Sorrow. The very sun and [[motifs/moon|moon]] seem taken from us. Outside, the day may be blue and gold, but the light that creeps down through the thickly-muffled glass of the small iron-barred [[motifs/window|window]] beneath which one sits is grey and niggard. It is always twilight in one’s cell, as it is always midnight in one’s heart. And in the sphere of thought, no less than in the sphere of [[motifs/time|time]], motion is no more. The thing that you personally have long ago forgotten, or can easily forget, is happening to me now, and will happen to me again to-morrow. Remember this, and you will be able to understand a little of why I am writing to you, and in this manner writing.
A week later, I am transferred here. Three more months go over and my mother dies. You knew, none better, how deeply I loved and honoured her. Her [[motifs/death|death]] was so terrible to me that I, once a lord of language, have no words in which to express my anguish and my shame. Never, even in the most perfect days of my development as an [[archetypes/artist|artist]], could I have had words fit to bear so august a burden, or to move with sufficient stateliness of music through the purple pageant of my incommunicable woe. She and my father had bequeathed me a name they had made noble and honoured not merely in Literature, Art, Archaeology and Science, but in the public [[concepts/history|history]] of my own country in its evolution as a nation. I had disgraced that name eternally. I had made it a low byword among low people. I had dragged it through the very mire. I had given it to brutes that they might make it brutal, and to fools that they might turn it into a synonym for folly. What I suffered then, and still suffer, is not for pen to write or paper to record. My wife, at that [[motifs/time|time]] kind and gentle to me, rather than that I should hear the news from indifferent or alien lips, travelled, ill as she was, all the way from Genoa to England to break to me herself the tidings of so irreparable, so irredeemable a loss. Messages of sympathy reached me from all who had still affection for me. Even people who had not known me personally, hearing what a new sorrow had come into my broken life, wrote to ask that some expression of their condolence should be conveyed to me. You alone stood aloof, sent me no message, and wrote me no letter. Of such actions, it is best to say what Virgil says to [[historical-references/dante|Dante]] of those whose lives have been barren in noble impulse and shallow of intention: ‘Non ragioniam di lor, ma guarda, epassa1.’
Three more months go over. The calendar of my daily conduct and labour that hangs on the outside of my cell-door, with my name and sentence written upon it, tells me that it is Maytime. My friends come to see me again. I enquire, as I always do, after you. I am told that you are in your villa at Naples, and are bringing out a volume of poems. At the close of the interview it is mentioned casually that you are dedicating them to me. The tidings seemed to give me a sort of nausea of life. I said nothing, but silently went back to my cell with contempt and scorn in my heart. How could you dream of dedicating a volume of poems to me without first asking my permission? Dream, do I say? How could you dare to do such a thing? Will you give as your answer that in the days of my [[concepts/greatness|greatness]] and fame I had consented to receive the dedication of your early work? Certainly, I did so; just as I would have accepted the homage of any other young man beginning the difficult and beautiful art of literature. All homage is delightful to an [[archetypes/artist|artist]], and doubly sweet when youth brings it. Laurel and bay leaf wither when aged hands pluck them. Only youth has a right to [[motifs/crown|crown]] an [[archetypes/artist|artist]]. That is the real privilege of being young, if youth only knew it. But the days of abasement and infamy are different from those of [[concepts/greatness|greatness]] and of fame. You have yet to learn that Prosperity, Pleasure and Success may be rough of grain and common in fibre, but that sorrow is the most sensitive of all created things. There is nothing that stirs in the whole world of thought or motion to which Sorrow does not vibrate in terrible if exquisite pulsation. The thin beaten-out leaf of tremulous gold that chronicles the direction of forces that the eye cannot see is in comparison coarse. It is a wound that bleeds when any hand but that of [[motifs/love|Love]] touches it and even then must bleed again, though not for [[motifs/pain|pain]].
You could write to the Governor of Wandsworth [[motifs/prison|Prison]] to ask my permission to publish my letters in the Mercure de France, ‘corresponding to our English Fortnightly Review.’ Why not have written to the Governor of the [[motifs/prison|Prison]] at Reading, to ask my permission to dedicate your poems to me, whatever fantastic description you may have chosen to give of them? Was it because in the one case the magazine in question had been prohibited by me from publishing the letters, the legal copyright of which, as you are of course perfectly [[motifs/well|well]] aware, was and is vested entirely in me, and in the other you thought that you could enjoy the wilfulness of your own way without my knowing anything about it till it was too late to interfere? The mere fact that I was a man disgraced, ruined, and in [[motifs/prison|prison]] should have made you, if you desired to write my name on the fore-page of your work, beg it of me as a favour, an [[concepts/honour|honour]], a privilege. That is the way in which one should approach those who are in distress and sit in shame.
Where there is Sorrow there is holy ground. Some day you will realise what that means. You will know nothing of life till you do. Robbie, and natures like his, can realise it. When I was brought down from my [[motifs/prison|prison]] to the [[settings/court|Court]] of Bankruptcy between two policemen, Robbie waited in the long dreary corridor, that before the whole crowd, whom an action so sweet and simple hushed into [[motifs/silence|silence]], he might gravely raise his hat to me, as handcuffed and with bowed head I passed him by. Men have gone to heaven for smaller things than that. It was in this spirit, and with this mode of [[motifs/love|love]] that the saints knelt down to wash the feet of the poor, or stooped to kiss the leper on the cheek. I have never said one single word to him about what he did. I do not know to the present moment whether he is aware that I was even conscious of his action. It is not a thing for which one can render formal thanks in formal words. I store it in the treasury-house of my heart. I keep it there as a secret debt that I am glad to think I can never possibly repay. It is embalmed and kept sweet by the myrrh and cassia of many [[motifs/tears|tears]]. When Wisdom has been profitless to me, and Philosophy barren, and the proverbs and phrases of those who have sought to give me consolation as [[motifs/dust|dust]] and ashes in my mouth, the [[motifs/memory|memory]] of that little lowly silent act of [[motifs/love|Love]] has unsealed for me all the wells of pity, made the [[motifs/desert|desert]] blossom like a [[motifs/rose|rose]], and brought me out of the bitterness of lonely [[concepts/exile|exile]] into harmony with the wounded, broken and great heart of the world. When you are able to understand, not merely how beautiful Robbie’s action was, but why it meant so much to me, and always will mean so much, then, perhaps, you will realise how and in what spirit you should have approached me for permission to dedicate to me your verses.
It is only right to state that in any case I would not have accepted the dedication. Though, possibly, it would under other circumstances have pleased me to have been asked, I would have refused the request for your sake, irrespective of any feelings of my own. The first volume of poems that in the very springtime of his manhood a young man sends forth to the world should be like a blossom or flower of [[motifs/spring|spring]], like the white thorn in the meadow at Magdalen, or the cowslips in the Cumnor fields. It should not be burdened by the weight of a terrible, a revolting [[forms/tragedy|tragedy]], a terrible, a revolting scandal. If I had allowed my name to serve as herald to the book it would have been a [[motifs/grave|grave]] artistic error. It would have brought a wrong atmosphere round the whole work, and in modern art atmosphere counts for so much. Modern life is complex and relative. Those are its two distinguishing notes. To render the first we require atmosphere with its subtlety of nuances, of suggestion, of strange perspectives: as for the second we require background. That is why Sculpture has ceased to be a representative art; and why Music is a representative art; and why Literature is, and has been, and always will remain the supreme representative art.
Your little book should have brought with it Sicilian and Arcadian airs, not the pestilent foulness of the criminal dock or the close breath of the convict cell. Nor would such a dedication as you proposed have been merely an error of taste in Art; it would from other points of view have been entirely unseemly. It would have looked like a continuance of your conduct before and after my arrest. It would have given people the impression of being an attempt at foolish bravado: an example of that kind of [[concepts/courage|courage]] that is sold cheap and bought cheap in the streets of shame. As far as our [[concepts/friendship|friendship]] is concerned Nemesis has crushed us both like flies. The dedication of verses to me when I was in [[motifs/prison|prison]] would have seemed a sort of silly effort at smart repartee, an accomplishment on which in your old days of dreadful letterwriting – days never, I sincerely [[concepts/hope|hope]] for your sake, to return – you used openly to [[concepts/pride|pride]] yourself and about which it was your joy to boast. It would not have produced the serious, the beautiful effect which I trust – I believe indeed – you had intended. Had you consulted me, I would have advised you to delay the publication of your verses for a little; or, if that proved displeasing to you, to publish anonymously at first, and then when you had won lovers by your song – the only sort of lovers really worth the winning – you might have turned round and said to the world ‘These [[motifs/flowers|flowers]] that you admire are of my sowing, and now I offer them to one whom you regard as a pariah and an outcast, as my tribute to what I [[motifs/love|love]] and reverence and admire in him.’ But you chose the wrong method and the wrong moment. There is a tact in [[motifs/love|love]], and a tact in literature: you were not sensitive to either.
I have spoken to you at length on this point in order that you should grasp its full bearings, and understand why I wrote at once to Robbie in terms of such scorn and contempt of you, and absolutely prohibited the dedication, and desired that the words I had written of you should be copied out carefully and sent to you. I felt that at last the [[motifs/time|time]] had come when you should be made to see, to recognise, to realise a little of what you had done. Blindness may be carried so far that it becomes [[concepts/grotesque|grotesque]], and an unimaginative [[concepts/nature|nature]], if something be not done to rouse it, will become petrified into absolute insensibility, so that while the [[concepts/body|body]] may eat, and drink, and have its pleasures, the [[motifs/soul|soul]], whose house it is, may, like the [[motifs/soul|soul]] of Branca d’Oria in [[historical-references/dante|Dante]], be dead absolutely. My letter seems to have arrived not a moment too soon. It fell on you, as far as I can judge, like a thunderbolt. You describe yourself, in your answer to Robbie, as being ‘deprived of all power of thought and expression.’ Indeed, apparently, you can think of nothing better than to write to your mother to complain. Of course, she, with that blindness to your real good that has been her ill-starred fortune and yours, gives you every comfort she can think of, and lulls you back, I suppose, into your former unhappy, unworthy condition; while as far as I am concerned, she lets my friends know that she is ‘very much annoyed’ at the severity of my remarks about you. Indeed it is not merely to my friends that she conveys her sentiments of annoyance, but also to those – a very much larger number, I need hardly remind you – who are not my friends: and I am informed now, and through channels very kindly-disposed to you and yours, that in consequence of this a great deal of the sympathy that, by reason of my distinguished genius and terrible sufferings, had been gradually but surely growing up for me, has been entirely taken away. People say ‘Ah! He first tried to get the kind father put into [[motifs/prison|prison]] and failed: now he turns round and blames the [[archetypes/innocent|innocent]] son for his failure. How right we were to despise him! How worthy of contempt he is!’ It seems to me that, when my name is mentioned in your mother’s presence, if she has no word of sorrow or regret for her share – no slight one – in the ruin of my house, it would be more seemly if she remained silent. And as for you – don’t you think now that, instead of writing to her to complain, it would have been better for you, in every way, to have written to me directly, and to have had the [[concepts/courage|courage]] to say to me whatever you had or fancied you had to say? It is nearly a year ago now since I wrote that letter. You cannot have remained during that entire [[motifs/time|time]] ‘deprived of all power of thought and expression.’ Why did you not write to me? You saw by my letter how deeply wounded, how outraged I was by your whole conduct. More than that; you saw your entire [[concepts/friendship|friendship]] with me set before you, at last, in its true light, and by a mode not to be mistaken. Often in old days I had told you that you were ruining my life. You had always laughed. When Edwin Levy at the very beginning of our [[concepts/friendship|friendship]], seeing your manner of putting me forward to bear the brunt, and annoyance, and expense even of that unfortunate [[settings/oxford|Oxford]] mishap of yours, if we must so term it, in reference to which his advice and help had been sought, warned me for the space of a whole hour against knowing you, you laughed, as at Bracknell I described to you my long and impressive interview with him. When I told you how even that unfortunate young man who ultimately stood beside me in the Dock had warned me more than once that you would prove far more fatal in bringing me to utter destruction than any even of the common lads whom I was foolish enough to know, you laughed, though not with such sense of amusement. When my more prudent or less [[motifs/well|well]]-disposed friends either warned me or left me, on account of my [[concepts/friendship|friendship]] with you, you laughed with scorn. You laughed immoderately when, on the occasion of your father writing his first abusive letter to you about me, I told you that I knew I would be the mere catspaw of your dreadful quarrel and come to some evil between you. But every single thing had happened as I had said it would happen, as far as the result goes. You had no excuse for not seeing how all things had come to pass. Why did you not write to me? Was it cowardice? Was it callousness? What was it? The fact that I was outraged with you, and had expressed my sense of outrage, was all the more reason for writing. If you thought my letter just, you should have written. If you thought it in the smallest point unjust, you should have written. I waited for a letter. I felt sure that at last you would see that, if old affection, much-protested [[motifs/love|love]], the thousand acts of ill-requited kindness I had showered on you, the thousand unpaid debts of gratitude you owed me – that if all these were nothing to you, mere duty itself, most barren of all bonds between man and man, should have made you write. You cannot say that you seriously thought I was obliged to receive none but business communications from members of my family. You knew perfectly [[motifs/well|well]] that every twelve weeks Robbie was writing to me a little budget of literary news. Nothing can be more charming than his letters, in their wit, their clever concentrated criticism, their light touch: they are real letters: they are like a person talking to one: they have the quality of a French causerie intime: and in his delicate modes of deference to me, appealing at one [[motifs/time|time]] to my judgment, at another to my sense of humour, at another to my instinct for [[concepts/beauty|beauty]] or to my culture, and reminding me in a hundred subtle ways that once I was to many an arbiter of style in Art, the supreme arbiter to some, he shows how he has the tact of [[motifs/love|love]] as [[motifs/well|well]] as the tact of literature. His letters have been the little messengers between me and that beautiful unreal world of Art where once I was [[archetypes/king|King]], and would have remained [[archetypes/king|King]], indeed, had I not let myself be lured into the imperfect world of coarse uncompleted passions, of appetite without distinction, [[motifs/desire|desire]] without limit, and formless [[concepts/greed|greed]]. Yet, when all is said, surely you might have been able to understand, or conceive, at any rate, in your own mind, that, even on the ordinary grounds of mere psychological curiosity, it would have been more interesting to me to hear from you than to learn that Alfred Austin was trying to bring out a volume of poems, or that Street was writing dramatic criticisms for the Daily Chronicle, or that by one who cannot speak a panegyric without stammering Mrs Meynell had been pronounced to be the new Sibyl of Style.
Ah! Had you been in [[motifs/prison|prison]] – I will not say through any fault of mine, for that would be a thought too terrible for me to bear – but through fault of your own, error of your own, [[concepts/faith|faith]] in some unworthy friend, slip in sensual mire, trust misapplied, or [[motifs/love|love]] ill-bestowed, or none, or all of these – do you think that I would have allowed you to eat your heart away in darkness and [[concepts/solitude|solitude]] without trying in some way, however slight, to help you to bear the bitter burden of your disgrace? Do you think that I would not have let you know that if you suffered, I was [[concepts/suffering|suffering]] too: that if you wept, there were [[motifs/tears|tears]] in my eyes also: and that if you lay in the house of bondage and were despised of men, I out of my griefs had built a house in which to dwell until your coming, a treasury in which all that men had denied to you would be laid up for your healing, one hundredfold in increase? If bitter necessity, or prudence, to me more bitter still, had prevented my being near you, and robbed me of the joy of your presence, though seen through [[motifs/prison|prison]]-bars and in a shape of shame, I would have written to you in season and out of season in the [[concepts/hope|hope]] that some mere phrase, some single word, some broken echo even of [[motifs/love|Love]] might reach you. If you had refused to receive my letters, I would have written none the less, so that you should have known that at any rate there were always letters waiting for you. Many have done so to me. Every three months people write to me, or propose to write to me. Their letters and communications are kept. They will be handed to me when I go out of [[motifs/prison|prison]]. I know that they are there. I know the names of the people who have written them. I know that they are full of sympathy, and affection, and kindness. That is sufficient for me. I need to know no more. Your [[motifs/silence|silence]] has been horrible. Nor has it been a [[motifs/silence|silence]] of weeks and months merely, but of years; of years even as they have to count them who, like yourself, live swiftly in happiness, and can hardly catch the gilt feet of the days as they [[motifs/dance|dance]] by, and are out of breath in the chase after pleasure. It is a [[motifs/silence|silence]] without excuse; a [[motifs/silence|silence]] without palliation. I knew you had feet of clay. Who knew it better? When I wrote, among my aphorisms, that it was simply the feet of clay that made the gold of the image precious, it was of you I was thinking. But it is no gold image with clay feet that you have made of yourself. Out of the very [[motifs/dust|dust]] of the common highway that the hooves of horned things pash into mire you have moulded your perfect semblance for me to look at, so that, whatever my secret [[motifs/desire|desire]] might have been, it would be impossible for me now to have for you any feeling other than that of contempt and scorn, for myself any feeling other than that of contempt and scorn either. And setting aside all other reasons, your indifference, your worldly wisdom, your callousness, your prudence, whatever you may choose to call it, has been made doubly bitter to me by the peculiar circumstances that either accompanied or followed my fall.
Other miserable men, when they are thrown into [[motifs/prison|prison]], if they are robbed of the [[concepts/beauty|beauty]] of the world, are at least safe, in some measure, from the world’s most deadly slings, most awful arrows. They can hide in the darkness of their cells, and of their very disgrace make a mode of sanctuary. The world, having had its will, goes its way, and they are left to suffer undisturbed. With me it has been different. Sorrow after sorrow has come beating at the [[motifs/prison|prison]] doors in search of me. They have opened the gates wide and let them in. Hardly, if at all, have my friends been suffered to see me. But my enemies have had full access to me always. Twice in my public appearances at the Bankruptcy [[settings/court|Court]], twice again in my public transferences from one [[motifs/prison|prison]] to another, have I been shown under conditions of unspeakable humiliation to the gaze and mockery of men. The messenger of [[motifs/death|Death]] has brought me his tidings and gone his way, and in entire [[concepts/solitude|solitude]], and isolated from all that could give me comfort, or suggest relief, I have had to bear the intolerable burden of misery and remorse that the [[motifs/memory|memory]] of my mother placed upon me, and places on me still. Hardly has that wound been dulled, not healed, by [[motifs/time|time]], when violent and bitter and harsh letters come to me from my wife through her solicitor. I am, at once, taunted and threatened with [[concepts/poverty|poverty]]. That I can bear. I can school myself to worse than that. But my two children are taken from me by legal procedure. That is and will remain to me a source of infinite distress, of infinite [[motifs/pain|pain]], of grief without end or limit. That the law should decide, and take upon itself to decide, that I am one unfit to be with my own children is something quite horrible to me. The disgrace of [[motifs/prison|prison]] is as nothing compared to it. I envy the other men who tread the yard along with me. I am sure that their children wait for them, look for their coming, will be sweet to them.
The poor are wiser, more charitable, more kind, more sensitive than we are. In their eyes [[motifs/prison|prison]] is a [[forms/tragedy|tragedy]] in a man’s life, a misfortune, a casualty, something that calls for sympathy in others. They speak of one who is in [[motifs/prison|prison]] as of one who is ‘in trouble’ simply. It is the phrase they always use, and the expression has the perfect wisdom of [[motifs/love|Love]] in it. With people of our rank it is different. With us [[motifs/prison|prison]] makes a man a pariah. I, and such as I am, have hardly any right to air and sun. Our presence taints the pleasures of others. We are unwelcome when we reappear. To revisit the glimpses of the [[motifs/moon|moon]] is not for us. Our very children are taken away. Those lovely links with humanity are broken. We are doomed to be solitary, while our sons still live. We are denied the one thing that might heal us and help us, that might bring balm to the bruised heart, and peace to the [[motifs/soul|soul]] in [[motifs/pain|pain]].
And to all this has been added the hard, small fact that by your actions and by your [[motifs/silence|silence]], by what you have done and by what you have left undone, you have made every day of my long [[concepts/imprisonment|imprisonment]] still more difficult for me to live through. The very bread and [[motifs/water|water]] of [[motifs/prison|prison]] fare you have by your conduct changed. You have rendered the one bitter and the other brackish to me. The sorrow you should have shared you have doubled, the [[motifs/pain|pain]] you should have sought to lighten you have quickened to anguish. I have no [[concepts/doubt|doubt]] that you did not mean to do so. I know that you did not mean to do so. It was simply that ‘one really fatal defect of your character, your entire lack of [[concepts/imagination|imagination]].’
And the end of it all is that I have got to forgive you. I must do so. I don’t write this letter to put bitterness into your heart, but to pluck it out of mine. For my own sake I must forgive you. One cannot always keep an adder in one’s breast to feed on one, nor rise up every [[motifs/night|night]] to sow thorns in the [[motifs/garden|garden]] of one’s [[motifs/soul|soul]]. It will not be difficult at all for me to do so, if you help me a little. Whatever you did to me in old days I always readily forgave. It did you no good then. Only one whose life is without stain of any kind can forgive sins. But now when I sit in humiliation and disgrace it is different. My [[concepts/forgiveness|forgiveness]] should mean a great deal to you now. Some day you will realise it. Whether you do so early or late, soon or not at all, my way is clear before me. I cannot allow you to go through life bearing in your heart the burden of having ruined a man like me. The thought might make you callously indifferent, or morbidly sad. I must take the burden from you and put it on my own shoulders.
I must say to myself that neither you nor your father, multiplied a thousand times over, could possibly have ruined a man like me: that I ruined myself: and that nobody, great or small, can be ruined except by his own hand. I am quite ready to do so. I am trying to do so, though you may not think it at the present moment. If I have brought this pitiless indictment against you, think what an indictment I bring without pity against myself. Terrible as what you did to me was, what I did to myself was far more terrible still.
I was a man who stood in symbolic relations to the art and culture of my age. I had realised this for myself at the very [[motifs/dawn|dawn]] of my manhood, and had forced my age to realise it afterwards. Few men hold such a position in their own lifetime and have it so acknowledged. It is usually discerned, if discerned at all, by the historian, or the critic, long after both the man and his age have passed away. With me it was different. I felt it myself, and made others feel it. Byron was a symbolic figure, but his relations were to the passion of his age and its weariness of passion. Mine were to something more noble, more permanent, of more vital issue, of larger scope.
The gods had given me almost everything. I had genius, a distinguished name, high social position, brilliancy, intellectual daring: I made art a philosophy, and philosophy an art: I altered the minds of men and the colours of things: there was nothing I said or did that did not make people [[concepts/wonder|wonder]]: I took the drama, the most objective form known to art, and made it as personal a mode of expression as the [[forms/lyric|lyric]] or the [[forms/sonnet|sonnet]], at the same [[motifs/time|time]] that I widened its range and enriched its characterisation: drama, [[forms/novel|novel]], poem in rhyme, poem in prose, subtle or fantastic dialogue, whatever I touched I made beautiful in a new mode of [[concepts/beauty|beauty]]: to [[concepts/truth|truth]] itself I gave what is false no less than what is true as its rightful province, and showed that the false and the true are merely forms of intellectual existence. I treated Art as the supreme reality, and life as a mere mode of fiction: I awoke the [[concepts/imagination|imagination]] of my century so that it created myth and legend around me: I summed up all systems in a phrase, and all existence in an [[forms/epigram|epigram]].
Along with these things, I had things that were different. I let myself be lured into long spells of senseless and sensual ease. I amused myself with being a flâneur, a dandy, a man of fashion. I surrounded myself with the smaller natures and the meaner minds. I became the spendthrift of my own genius, and to waste an eternal youth gave me a curious joy. Tired of being on the heights I deliberately went to the depths in the search for new sensations. What the [[concepts/paradox|paradox]] was to me in the sphere of thought, perversity became to me in the sphere of passion. [[motifs/desire|Desire]], at the end, was a malady, or a [[concepts/madness|madness]], or both. I grew careless of the lives of others. I took pleasure where it pleased me and passed on. I forgot that every little action of the common day makes or unmakes character, and that therefore what one has done in the secret chamber one has some day to cry aloud on the housetops. I ceased to be Lord over myself. I was no longer the Captain of my [[motifs/soul|Soul]], and did not know it. I allowed you to dominate me, and your father to frighten me. I ended in horrible disgrace. There is only one thing for me now, absolute [[concepts/humility|Humility]]: just as there is only one thing for you, absolute [[concepts/humility|Humility]] also. You had better come down into the [[motifs/dust|dust]] and learn it beside me.
I have lain in [[motifs/prison|prison]] for nearly two years. Out of my [[concepts/nature|nature]] has come wild [[concepts/despair|despair]]; an abandonment to grief that was piteous even to look at: terrible and impotent rage: bitterness and scorn: anguish that wept aloud: misery that could find no voice: sorrow that was dumb. I have passed through every possible mood of [[concepts/suffering|suffering]]. Better than Wordsworth himself I know what Wordsworth meant when he said:
 [[concepts/suffering|Suffering]] is permanent, obscure, and dark
And has the [[concepts/nature|nature]] of Infinity.
 But while there were times when I rejoiced in the idea that my sufferings were to be endless, I could not bear them to be without meaning. Now I find hidden away in my [[concepts/nature|nature]] something that tells me that nothing in the whole world is meaningless, and [[concepts/suffering|suffering]] least of all. That something hidden away in my [[concepts/nature|nature]], like a treasure in a field, is [[concepts/humility|Humility]].
It is the last thing left in me, and the best: the ultimate discovery at which I have arrived: the starting-point for a fresh development. It has come to me right out of myself, so I know that it has come at the proper [[motifs/time|time]]. It could not have come before, nor later. Had anyone told me of it, I would have rejected it. Had it been brought to me, I would have refused it. As I found it, I want to keep it. I must do so. It is the one thing that has in it the elements of life, of a new life, a Vita Nuova for me. Of all things it is the strangest. One cannot give it away, and another may not give it to one. One cannot acquire it, except by surrendering everything that one has. It is only when one has lost all things, that one knows that one possesses it.
Now that I realise that it is in me, I see quite clearly what I have got to do, what, in fact, I must do. And when I use such a phrase as that, I need not tell you that I am not alluding to any external sanction or command. I admit none. I am far more of an individualist than I ever was. Nothing seems to me of the smallest value except what one gets out of oneself. My [[concepts/nature|nature]] is seeking a fresh mode of [[concepts/self|self]]-realisation. That is all I am concerned with. And the first thing that I have got to do is to free myself from any possible bitterness of feeling against you.
I am completely penniless, and absolutely homeless. Yet there are worse things in the world than that. I am quite candid when I tell you that rather than go out from this [[motifs/prison|prison]] with bitterness in my heart against you or against the world I would gladly and readily beg my bread from door to door. If I got nothing from the house of the rich, I would get something at the house of the poor. Those who have much are often greedy. Those who have little always share. I would not a bit mind sleeping in the cool grass in [[motifs/summer|summer]], and when [[motifs/winter|winter]] came on sheltering myself by the warm close-thatched rick, or under the penthouse of a great barn, provided I had [[motifs/love|love]] in my heart. The external things of life seem to me now of no importance at all. You can see to what intensity of [[concepts/individualism|individualism]] I have arrived, or am arriving rather, for the [[motifs/journey|journey]] is long, and ‘where I walk there are thorns.’
Of course I know that to ask for alms on the highway is not to be my lot, and that if ever I lie in the cool grass at [[motifs/night|night]]-[[motifs/time|time]] it will be to write sonnets to the [[motifs/moon|Moon]]. When I go out of [[motifs/prison|prison]], Robbie will be waiting for me on the other side of the big iron-studded gate, and he is the symbol not merely of his own affection, but of the affection of many others besides. I believe I am to have enough to live on for about eighteen months at any rate, so that, if I may not write beautiful books, I may at least read beautiful books, and what joy can be greater? After that, I [[concepts/hope|hope]] to be able to recreate my creative faculty. But were things different: had I not a friend left in the world: were there not a single house open to me even in pity: had I to accept the wallet and ragged cloak of sheer penury: still as long as I remained free from all resentment, hardness, and scorn, I would be able to face life with much more calm and confidence than I would were my [[concepts/body|body]] in purple and fine linen, and the [[motifs/soul|soul]] within it sick with hate. And I shall really have no difficulty in forgiving you. But to make it a pleasure for me you must feel that you want it. When you really want it you will find it waiting for you.
I need not say that my task does not end there. It would be comparatively easy if it did. There is much more before me. I have hills far steeper to climb, valleys much darker to pass through. And I have to get it all out of myself. Neither Religion, Morality, nor Reason can help me at all.
Morality does not help me. I am a born antinomian. I am one of those who are made for exceptions, not for laws. But while I see that there is nothing wrong in what one does, I see that there is something wrong in what one becomes. It is [[motifs/well|well]] to have learned that.
Religion does not help me. The [[concepts/faith|faith]] that others give to what is unseen, I give to what one can touch, and look at. My Gods dwell in temples made with hands, and within the circle of actual experience is my creed made perfect and complete: too complete it may be, for like many or all of those who have placed their Heaven in this earth, I have found in it not merely the [[concepts/beauty|beauty]] of Heaven, but the horror of Hell also. When I think about Religion at all, I feel as if I would like to found an order for those who cannot believe: the Confraternity of the Fatherless one might call it, where on an altar, on which no taper burned, a priest, in whose heart peace had no dwelling, might celebrate with unblessed bread and a chalice empty of wine. Everything to be true must become a religion. And agnosticism should have its ritual no less than [[concepts/faith|faith]]. It has sown its martyrs, it should reap its saints, and praise God daily for having hidden Himself from man. But whether it be [[concepts/faith|faith]] or agnosticism, it must be nothing external to me. Its symbols must be of my own creating. Only that is spiritual which makes its own form. If I may not find its secret within myself, I shall never find it. If I have not got it already, it will never come to me.
Reason does not help me. It tells me that the laws under which I am convicted are wrong and unjust laws, and the system under which I have suffered a wrong and unjust system. But, somehow, I have got to make both of these things just and right to me. And exactly as in Art one is only concerned with what a particular thing is at a particular moment to oneself, so it is also in the ethical evolution of one’s character. I have got to make everything that has happened to me good for me. The plank-bed, the loathsome food, the hard ropes shredded into oakum till one’s finger-tips grow dull with [[motifs/pain|pain]], the menial offices with which each day begins and finishes, the harsh orders that routine seems to necessitate, the dreadful dress that makes sorrow [[concepts/grotesque|grotesque]] to look at, the [[motifs/silence|silence]], the [[concepts/solitude|solitude]], the shame – each and all of these things I have to transform into a spiritual experience. There is not a single degradation of the [[concepts/body|body]] which I must not try and make into a spiritualising of the [[motifs/soul|soul]].
I want to get to the point when I shall be able to say, quite simply and without affectation, that the two great turning-points of my life were when my father sent me to [[settings/oxford|Oxford]], and when society sent me to [[motifs/prison|prison]]. I will not say that it is the best thing that could have happened to me, for that phrase would savour of too great bitterness towards myself. I would sooner say, or hear it said of me, that I was so typical a [[archetypes/child|child]] of my age that in my perversity, and for that perversity’s sake, I turned the good things of my life to evil, and the evil things of my life to good. What is said, however, by myself or by others matters little. The important thing, the thing that lies before me, the thing that I have to do, or be for the brief remainder of my days one maimed, marred, and incomplete, is to absorb into my [[concepts/nature|nature]] all that has been done to me, to make it part of me, to accept it without complaint, [[concepts/fear|fear]], or reluctance. The supreme vice is shallowness. Whatever is realised is right.
When first I was put into [[motifs/prison|prison]] some people advised me to try and forget who I was. It was ruinous advice. It is only by realising what I am that I have found comfort of any kind. Now I am advised by others to try on my release to forget that I have ever been in a [[motifs/prison|prison]] at all. I know that would be equally fatal. It would mean that I would be always haunted by an intolerable sense of disgrace, and that those things that are meant as much for me as for anyone else – the [[concepts/beauty|beauty]] of the sun and [[motifs/moon|moon]], the pageant of the [[motifs/seasons|seasons]], the music of daybreak and the [[motifs/silence|silence]] of great nights, the rain falling through the [[motifs/leaves|leaves]], or the dew creeping over the grass and making it silver – would all be tainted for me, and lose their healing power and their power of communicating joy. To reject one’s own experiences is to arrest one’s own development. To deny one’s own experiences is to put a lie into the lips of one’s own life. It is no less than a denial of the [[motifs/soul|Soul]]. For just as the [[concepts/body|body]] absorbs things of all kinds, things common and unclean no less than those that the priest or a vision has cleansed, and converts them into swiftness or strength, into the play of beautiful muscles and the moulding of fair flesh, into the curves and colours of the hair, the lips, the eye: so the [[motifs/soul|Soul]], in its turn, has its nutritive functions also, and can transform into noble moods of thought, and passions of high import, what in itself is base, cruel, and degrading: nay more, may find in these its most august modes of assertion, and can often reveal itself most perfectly through what was intended to desecrate or destroy.
The fact of my having been the common prisoner of a common gaol I must frankly accept, and, curious as it may seem to you, one of the things I shall have to teach myself is not to be ashamed of it. I must accept it as a punishment, and if one is ashamed of having been punished, one might just as [[motifs/well|well]] never have been punished at all. Of course there are many things of which I was convicted that I had not done, but then there are many things of which I was convicted that I had done, and a still greater number of things in my life for which I was never indicted at all. And as for what I have said in this letter, that the gods are strange, and punish us for what is good and humane in us as much as for what is evil and perverse, I must accept the fact that one is punished for the good as [[motifs/well|well]] as for the evil that one does. I have no [[concepts/doubt|doubt]] that it is quite right one should be. It helps one, or should help one, to realise both, and not to be too conceited about either. And if I then am not ashamed of my punishment, as I [[concepts/hope|hope]] not to be, I shall be able to think, and walk, and live with [[concepts/freedom|freedom]].
Many men on their release carry their [[motifs/prison|prison]] along with them into the air, hide it as a secret disgrace in their hearts, and at length like poor poisoned things creep into some hole and die. It is wretched that they should have to do so, and it is wrong, terribly wrong, of Society that it should force them to do so. Society takes upon itself the right to inflict appalling punishments on the individual, but it also has the supreme vice of shallowness, and fails to realise what it has done. When the man’s punishment is over, it [[motifs/leaves|leaves]] him to himself: that is to say it abandons him at the very moment when its highest duty towards him begins. It is really ashamed of its own actions, and shuns those whom it has punished, as people shun a creditor whose debt they cannot pay, or one on whom they have inflicted an irreparable, an irredeemable wrong. I claim on my side that if I realise what I have suffered, Society should realise what it has inflicted on me: and that there should be no bitterness or hate on either side.
Of course I know that from one point of view things will be made more difficult for me than for others; must indeed, by the very [[concepts/nature|nature]] of the case, be made so. The poor thieves and outcasts who are imprisoned here with me are in many respects more fortunate than I am. The little way in grey [[motifs/city|city]] or green field that saw their sin is small: to find those who know nothing of what they have done they need go no further than a bird might fly between the twilight before [[motifs/dawn|dawn]] and [[motifs/dawn|dawn]] itself: but for me ‘the world is shrivelled to a hands-breadth,’ and everywhere I turn my name is written on the rocks in lead. For I have come, not from obscurity into the momentary notoriety of crime, but from a sort of [[concepts/eternity|eternity]] of fame to a sort of [[concepts/eternity|eternity]] of infamy, and sometimes seem to myself to have shown, if indeed it required showing, that between the famous and the infamous there is but one step, if so much as one.
Still, in the very fact that people will recognise me wherever I go, and know all about my life, as far as its follies go, I can discern something good for me. It will force on me the necessity of again asserting myself as an [[archetypes/artist|artist]], and as soon as I possibly can. If I can produce one more beautiful work of art I shall be able to rob malice of its venom, and cowardice of its sneer, and to pluck out the tongue of scorn by the roots. And if life be, as it surely is, a problem to me, I am no less a problem to Life. People must adopt some attitude towards me, and so pass judgment both on themselves and me. I need not say I am not talking of particular individuals. The only people I would care to be with now are artists and people who have suffered: those who know what [[concepts/beauty|Beauty]] is, and those who know what Sorrow is: nobody else interests me. Nor am I making any demands on Life. In all that I have said I am simply concerned with my own mental attitude towards life as a whole: and I feel that not to be ashamed of having been punished is one of the first points I must attain to, for the sake of my own perfection, and because I am so imperfect.
Then I must learn how to be happy. Once I knew it, or thought I knew it, by instinct. It was always springtime once in my heart. My temperament was akin to joy. I filled my life to the very brim with pleasure, as one might fill a cup to the very brim with wine. Now I am approaching life from a completely new standpoint, and even to conceive happiness is often extremely difficult for me. I remember during my first term at [[settings/oxford|Oxford]] reading in Pater’s [[historical-references/renaissance|Renaissance]] – that book which has had such a strange influence over my life – how [[historical-references/dante|Dante]] places low in the Inferno those who wilfully live in sadness, and going to the College Library and turning to the passage in the Divine [[forms/comedy|Comedy]] where beneath the dreary marsh lie those who were ‘sullen in the sweet air,’ saying for ever through their sighs:
Tristi fummo
nell’aer dolce che dal sol s’allegra.1
 I knew the church condemned accidia,2 but the whole idea seemed to me quite fantastic, just the sort of sin, I fancied, a priest who knew nothing about real life would invent. Nor could I understand how [[historical-references/dante|Dante]], who says that ‘sorrow remarries us to God,’ could have been so harsh to those who were enamoured of [[concepts/melancholy|melancholy]], if any such there really were. I had no idea that some day this would become to me one of the greatest temptations of my life.
While I was in Wandsworth [[motifs/prison|Prison]] I longed to die. It was my one [[motifs/desire|desire]]. When after two months in the Infirmary I was transferred here, and found myself growing gradually better in physical health, I was filled with rage. I determined to commit [[concepts/suicide|suicide]] on the very day on which I left [[motifs/prison|prison]]. After a [[motifs/time|time]] that evil mood passed away, and I made up my mind to live, but to wear gloom as a [[archetypes/king|King]] wears purple: never to smile again: to turn whatever house I entered into a house of mourning: to make my friends walk slowly in sadness with me: to teach them that [[concepts/melancholy|melancholy]] is the true secret of life: to maim them with an alien sorrow: to mar them with my own [[motifs/pain|pain]]. Now I feel quite differently. I see it would be both ungrateful and unkind of me to pull so long a face that when my friends came to see me they would have to make their faces still longer in order to show their sympathy, or, if I desired to entertain them, to invite them to sit down silently to bitter herbs and funeral baked meats. I must learn how to be cheerful and happy.
The last two occasions on which I was allowed to see my friends here I tried to be as cheerful as possible, and to show my cheerfulness in order to make them some slight return for their trouble in coming all the way from town to visit me. It is only a slight return, I know, but it is the one, I feel certain, that pleases them most. I saw Robbie for an hour on Saturday week, and I tried to give the fullest possible expression to the delight I really felt at our meeting. And that, in the views and ideas I am here shaping for myself, I am quite right is shown to me by the fact that now for the first [[motifs/time|time]] since my [[concepts/imprisonment|imprisonment]] I have a real [[motifs/desire|desire]] to live.
There is before me so much to do that I would regard it as a terrible [[forms/tragedy|tragedy]] if I died before I was allowed to complete at any rate a little of it. I see new developments in Art and Life, each one of which is a fresh mode of perfection. I long to live so that I can explore what is no less than a new world to me. Do you want to know what this new world is? I think you can guess what it is. It is the world in which I have been living.
Sorrow, then, and all that it teaches one, is my new world. I used to live entirely for pleasure. I shunned sorrow and [[concepts/suffering|suffering]] of every kind. I hated both. I resolved to ignore them as far as possible, to treat them, that is to say, as modes of imperfection. They were not part of my scheme of life. They had no place in my philosophy. My mother, who knew life as a whole, used often to quote to me Goethe’s lines – written by Carlyle in a book he had given her years ago – and translated, I fancy, by him also:

<<<<<<< HEAD

<span class="qlang-split" data-lang="it"></span>

(Dal latino “dalle profondità”.)  
Questa lettera fu scritta da Wilde durante la sua [[concepts/imprisonment|carcerazione]] nel carcere di Reading Gaol per Lord Alfred Douglas. Nella prima metà, Wilde racconta della loro precedente relazione e dello stile di vita stravagante che alla fine portò alla sua condanna e [[concepts/imprisonment|carcerazione]] per oscenità gravissime. Denuncia sia la [[concepts/vanity|vanità]] di Lord Alfred che la propria debolezza nel cedere a tali desideri. Nella seconda metà, descrive il proprio sviluppo spirituale in prigione e l’identificazione con Gesù [[characters/christ|Cristo]], che definisce un artista romantico e individualista.  
Wilde scrisse la lettera tra gennaio e marzo del 1897, verso la fine della sua [[concepts/imprisonment|carcerazione]]. Il drammaturgo soffriva a causa del lavoro fisico e dell’isolamento emotivo. Un nuovo direttore del carcere ritenne che scrivere potesse rappresentare un modo più terapeutico rispetto al lavoro forzato. L’opera di Wilde fu attentamente supervisionata e non gli fu permesso inviarla; la portò con sé al momento della liberazione e la affidò a Robert Ross, chiedendogli di farne fare due copie: una da inviare a lui stesso e l’altra a Douglas. Ross pubblicò la lettera nel 1905, cinque anni dopo la morte di Wilde, dando al testo il titolo “De Profundis”, tratto dal Salmo 130. Si trattava però di una versione incompleta, priva degli elementi autobiografici; diverse edizioni successive ripristinarono parte del contenuto originale fino a quando, nel 1962, il testo completo fu pubblicato in un volume delle lettere di Wilde.

Robert Ross nel 1911. Fu l’esecutore letterario di Wilde e si occupò della pubblicazione di “De Profundis”.

**DALL’ABISSO**  
**Epistola: in prigione e incatenato**  
H.M., [[motifs/prison|Prigione]], Reading  
Gennaio-Marzo 1897 Caro Bosie, Dopo una lunga attesa infruttuosa, ho deciso di scriverti personalmente, sia per il tuo bene che per il mio. Non vorrei mai pensare di aver trascorso due anni interi in [[concepts/imprisonment|prigione]] senza ricevere nemmeno una tua lettera, né alcuna notizia o messaggio, se non quelli che mi causavano solo [[motifs/pain|dolore]]. La nostra sfortunata e molto lamentevole [[concepts/friendship|amicizia]] è finita in rovina e nella pubblica infamia per me; tuttavia il ricordo del nostro antico affetto mi accompagna ancora spesso. Il pensiero che odio, amarezza e disprezzo possano sostituire per sempre nell’ mio cuore ciò che un tempo era [[motifs/love|amore]] è davvero molto triste. E credo anche tu senta nel tuo cuore che scrivere a me, mentre vivo nella solitudine della prigione, sia meglio che pubblicare le mie lettere senza il mio permesso, o dedicarmi poesie senza essere stato invitato. Anche se il mondo non saprà mai nulla delle parole di dolore, passione, rimorso o indifferenza che sceglierai di inviarmi in risposta o come appello.
Non ho alcun [[concepts/doubt|dubbio]] che in questa lettera, nella quale devo parlare della tua vita e della mia, del passato e del futuro, di cose dolci trasformate in amarezza e di cose amare che potrebbero diventare gioia, ci sarà molto che ferirà profondamente la tua [[concepts/vanity|vanità]]. Se così dovesse essere, leggi questa lettera più e più volte, fino a quando non avrà distrutto completamente la tua vanità. Se trovi in essa qualcosa per cui senti di essere ingiustamente accusato, ricorda che si dovrebbe essere grati anche solo per un errore per il quale si può essere accusati ingiustamente. Se c’è una sola frase in questa lettera che ti fa venire le [[motifs/tears|lacrime]] agli occhi, piangi, proprio come piangiamo nella [[motifs/prison|prigione]], dove sia di giorno che di notte ci sono momenti dedicati alle lacrime. È l’unica cosa che può salvarti. Se vai a lamentarti con tua madre, come hai fatto riguardo al disprezzo che ho mostrato per te nella mia lettera a Robbie, affinché lei possa lusingarti e consolarti, ritornando così alla [[concepts/self|autoindulgenza]] o all’arroganza, sarai completamente perduto. Se trovi anche solo una scusa falsa per te stesso, presto ne troverai cento, e tornerai esattamente come eri prima. Continui ancora a dire, come hai detto a Robbie nella tua risposta, che “attribuisco motivi indegni” a te? Ah! Nella tua vita non c’erano motivi veri; avevi solo desideri. Un motivo è uno scopo intellettuale. Il fatto che fossi “molto giovane” quando è iniziata la nostra [[concepts/friendship|amicizia]]? Il tuo difetto non era quello di sapere troppo poco della vita, ma piuttosto di saperne troppo. La mattina dell’infanzia, con il suo delicato splendore, la sua luce chiara e pura, la gioia dell’innocenza e delle aspettative, tu l’avevi già lasciata molto indietro. Con passi veloci e decisi eri passato dal romanzo al realismo; i vicoli bui e le cose che vi vivono avevano iniziato a affascinarti. È da lì che sono nati i problemi per cui hai chiesto il mio aiuto, e io, seguendo la saggezza di questo mondo, per pietà e gentilezza te l’ho dato. Devi leggere questa lettera tutta d’un fiato, anche se ogni parola potrebbe ferirti profondamente, come il [[motifs/fire|fuoco]] o il coltello del chirurgo che fa sanguinare la carne delicata. Ricorda che il [[archetypes/fool|stupido]] agli occhi degli dei è molto diverso da quello agli occhi dell’uomo: una persona che ignora completamente le forme dell’arte nel suo sviluppo, i modi di pensiero nel loro progresso, la magnificenza della poesia latina o la ricchezza della musica greca, la scultura toscana o il canto elisabettiano, può comunque essere piena della più dolce saggezza. Il vero [[archetypes/fool|stupido]], quello che gli dei deridono e distruggono, è colui che non conosce se stesso. Anch’io sono stato così per molto tempo; tu lo sei stato anch’tu per molto tempo. Non essere più così. Non avere paura: il vizio supremo è la superficialità. Tutto ciò che viene realizzato è giusto. Ricorda anche che qualsiasi cosa ti sia dolorosa da leggere, è ancora più doloroso per me scriverla. Per te, le Forze Invisibili sono state molto gentili: ti hanno permesso di vedere le forme strane e tragiche della vita, proprio come si vedono le ombre in un cristallo.
La testa di Medusa che trasforma gli uomini vivi in pietra. A te è stato permesso di guardarla soltanto attraverso uno [[motifs/mirror|specchio]]. Tu stessa hai camminato liberamente tra i [[motifs/flowers|fiori]]. A me, invece, è stato tolto il bellissimo mondo dei colori e del movimento.
Comincerò dicendovi che mi biasimo terribilmente. Mentre sono seduto qui in questa cella buia, vestito come un prigioniero, un uomo disonorato e distrutto, è a me stesso che imputo tutto. Nei notti agitate e tormentate, nei giorni lunghi e monotoni di [[motifs/pain|dolore]], sono sempre io il responsabile. Mi biasimo per aver permesso a un’[[concepts/friendship|amicizia]] non intellettuale, a un’amicizia il cui scopo principale non era certo la creazione e la contemplazione di cose belle, di dominare completamente la mia vita. Fin dall’inizio c’era un abisso troppo grande tra noi: tu eri negligente a scuola, ancora peggio all’università. Non capivi che un [[archetypes/artist|artista]], soprattutto uno come me, il cui lavoro dipende dall’intensificazione della propria personalità, ha bisogno per lo sviluppo della sua arte dell’accompagnamento di idee, di un ambiente intellettuale, di tranquillità, di pace e di [[concepts/solitude|solitudine]]. Ammiravi il mio lavoro quando era finito; ti divertivi ai grandi successi delle mie prime opere e alle feste che ne seguivano; eri orgoglioso, e naturalmente lo eri, di essere l’amico intimo di un [[archetypes/artist|artista]] così distinto. Ma non riuscivi a comprendere le condizioni necessarie per la creazione artistica. Non parlo con frasi retoriche o esagerazioni: dico la pura [[concepts/truth|verità]], quando ricordo che in tutto il tempo in cui siamo stati insieme, non ho scritto nemmeno una riga. Che fosse a Torquay, Goring, [[settings/london|Londra]], Firenze o altrove. La mia vita, finché eri al mio fianco, è stata completamente sterile e priva di creatività. E, purtroppo, quasi sempre sei stato al mio fianco.
Ricordo, ad esempio, a settembre del ’93: per citare solo un esempio tra tanti, presi un gruppo di stanze puramente per poter lavorare in tranquillità, poiché avevo rotto il contratto con John Hare, al quale avevo promesso di scrivere una pièce e che insisteva molto sull’argomento. Nella prima settimana te ne stavi lontano. Naturalmente, eravamo in disaccordo sulla valutazione artistica della tua traduzione di “Salomè”; quindi ti limitasti a mandarmi lettere stupide sull’argomento. In quella settimana scrissi e completai ogni dettaglio del primo atto di “An Ideal Husband”, così come fu poi rappresentato. Nella seconda settimana tornasti, e il mio lavoro dovette praticamente essere abbandonato. Arrivavo ogni mattina alle 11:30 a St James’s Place per poter pensare e scrivere in tranquillità, lontano dalle interruzioni legate alla mia stessa casa, così tranquilla e serena com’era. Ma tutto fu inutile. Alle dodici arrivavi tu, restavi lì a fumare sigarette e chiacchierare fino alle 13:30, quando dovevo portarti a pranzo al Café Royal o al Berkeley. Il pranzo, con i suoi liquori, durava di solito fino alle 15:30. Poi te ne andavi da White’s per un’ora. Al tè ricomparivi e restavi fino all’ora di vestirsi per la cena. Cenavi con me al Savoy o in Tite Street. Di solito non ci separavamo prima di mezzanotte, poiché la cena da Willis’s concludeva quella giornata “incantevole”. Questa era la mia vita per quei tre mesi, ogni singolo giorno, tranne che nei quattro giorni in cui te ne andavi all’estero. In quei casi dovevo naturalmente recarmi a Calais per riportarti indietro. Per la mia natura e il mio temperamento, quella era una situazione al tempo stesso grottesca e tragica. Sicuramente ora lo capisci, vero? Devi renderti conto che la tua incapacità di stare da solo, la tua natura estremamente esigente nel richiedere costantemente l’attenzione e il tempo degli altri, la tua mancanza di capacità di concentrarti intellettualmente per lunghi periodi. Quell’incidente sfortunato – poiché preferisco pensare che non fosse altro che un incidente – il fatto che tu non fossi ancora riuscito ad acquisire quella “temperatura intellettuale tipica di Oxford”. Il fatto che tu non fossi mai stato in grado di affrontare argomenti intellettuali con serenità, ma arrivassi sempre a conclusioni drastiche. Tutto questo, unito al fatto che i tuoi desideri e interessi riguardavano la vita quotidiana piuttosto che l’arte. Era altrettanto dannoso per il tuo progresso culturale quanto lo era per il mio lavoro di artista. Quando paragono la mia amicizia con te a quella con giovani come John Gray e Pierre Louÿs, mi sento in imbarazzo. La mia vera vita, la mia vita più significativa, l’ho trascorsa con loro.
Dei terribili risultati della mia [[concepts/friendship|amicizia]] con te non parlerò ora. Penso soltanto alla sua qualità durante il periodo in cui è durata. Per me è stata intellettualmente degradante. In te germogliavano i rudimenti di un temperamento artistico, ma ti ho incontrato troppo tardi, o forse troppo presto, non so. Quando eri lontana, andava tutto bene. Nel dicembre di quell’anno, quando sono riuscito a convincere tua madre a farti lasciare l’Inghilterra, ho ripreso in mano il “tessuto strappato e disordinato” della mia [[concepts/imagination|immaginazione]], ho ripreso il controllo della mia vita. Non solo ho completato gli ultimi tre atti di *An Ideal Husband*, ma ho anche concepito e quasi terminato altre due opere di genere completamente diverso: la tragedia fiorentina *La Sainte Courtisane*. Ma all’improvviso, senza essere stato invitato, in circostanze fatali per la mia felicità, sei tornata. Le due opere che avevo lasciato incompiute non sono mai state riprese. Quell’umore creativo non è più stato possibile ritrovare. Ora che hai pubblicato tu stessa un volume di poesie, sarai in grado di riconoscere la [[concepts/truth|verità]] di tutto ciò che ho detto. Che tu possa farlo o meno, questa verità orribile rimane nel cuore della nostra [[concepts/friendship|amicizia]]. Mentre eri con me, sei stata la rovina assoluta della mia arte. Per aver permesso che tu ti frapponessi costantemente tra l’arte e me, mi sono inflitto vergogna e rimorso in tutto il loro pieno. Non potevi capire. Non avevi alcuna capacità di comprendere. Non avevo il diritto di aspettarmelo da te. I tuoi interessi riguardavano soltanto i pasti e le tue umore. I tuoi desideri erano legati a divertimenti, a piaceri ordinari o meno. Erano ciò che il tuo temperamento richiedeva. O almeno così pensavi in quel momento. Avrei dovuto vietarti l’accesso alla mia casa e alle mie stanze, tranne quando te lo chiedevo io stesso. Mi biasimo senza riserve per la mia debolezza. Era soltanto debolezza. Per me, mezz’ora dedicata all’arte valeva più di un’intera giornata trascorsa con te. Nella mia vita, nulla ha mai avuto la minima importanza rispetto all’arte. Ma per un [[archetypes/artist|artista]], la debolezza è quasi un crimine. Quando quella debolezza paralizza l’[[concepts/imagination|immaginazione]].
Mi biasimo ancora una volta per aver permesso che tu mi portassi a una rovina finanziaria totale e disonorevole. Ricordo una mattina all’inizio di ottobre del ’92: ero seduto nei boschi ingialliti di Bracknell insieme a tua madre. A quel tempo sapevo molto poco della tua vera natura. Ero stato da te ad [[settings/oxford|Oxford]] dal sabato al lunedì; tu eri rimasta con me a Cromer per dieci giorni e avevamo giocato a golf. La conversazione si concentrò su di te, e tua madre iniziò a parlarmi del tuo carattere. Mi raccontò dei tuoi due principali difetti: la tua [[concepts/vanity|vanità]] e il fatto che, secondo lei, “non capissi affatto cosa significasse gestire i soldi”. Ricordo chiaramente di aver riso. Non avevo idea che quella vanità mi avrebbe portato in prigione e quell’eccessiva spensieratezza alla bancarotta. Pensavo che la vanità fosse una sorta di “fiore elegante” da possedere da giovane; quanto all’extravaganza, be’, credevo che si trattasse semplicemente di un comportamento eccessivo. La prudenza e l’economia non facevano parte della mia natura, né della mia cultura. Ma prima ancora che la nostra amicizia durasse un mese, iniziai a capire cosa tua madre intendesse davvero. La tua insistenza nel vivere in modo dispendioso, le tue continue richieste di denaro, il fatto che pretendessi che tutti i tuoi piaceri fossero pagati da me, indipendentemente dal fatto che fossi con te o no. Questo mi causò seri problemi finanziari. Inoltre, il modo in cui insistevi costantemente per controllare la mia vita rendeva quelle spese ancora più insopportabili. Il denaro veniva quasi interamente speso in cose banali come cibo e bevande. A volte è piacevole avere una tavola imbandita con vino e fiori, ma tu andavi ben oltre ogni limite di gusto e moderazione. Chiedevi senza ritegno e accettavi tutto senza ringraziamenti. Iniziasti a pensare di avere il diritto di vivere alle mie spalle, in un lusso a cui non eri abituata. E questo rendeva i tuoi desideri ancora più intensi. Quando perdevi soldi al gioco d’azzardo in qualche casinò di Algeri, semplicemente mi telegrafavi il giorno dopo chiedendomi di coprire le perdite sul tuo conto bancario, senza nemmeno pensarci più.
Quando ti dico che, tra l’[[motifs/autumn|autunno]] del 1892 e la data della mia [[concepts/imprisonment|detenzione]], ho speso con te e per te più di 5000 sterline in denaro effettivo, senza considerare le spese che ho sostenuto, allora puoi farti un’idea del tipo di vita a cui tu insistevi. Pensi che esageri? Le mie spese ordinarie con te, per una giornata normale a [[settings/london|Londra]] – pranzo, cena, divertimenti, taxi e tutto il resto – andavano da 12 a 20 sterline; le spese settimanali, naturalmente, erano proporzionali e variavano da 80 a 130 sterline. Nei nostri tre mesi a Goring, le mie spese (inclusa l’affitto) ammontarono a 1340 sterline. Step by step, insieme al liquidatore fallimentare, ho dovuto esaminare ogni singola spesa della mia vita. È stato orribile. “Vita semplice e pensieri elevati” era, ovviamente, un ideale che tu non potevi apprezzare in quel momento; ma una tale stravaganza rappresentava una vergogna per entrambi. Uno dei pasti più deliziosi che ricordo di aver mangiato è stato quello che ho condiviso con Robbie in un piccolo caffè di Soho. Il costo di quel pasto era all’incirca lo stesso delle cene che spendevo con te, ma in sterline. Dal mio incontro con Robbie è nato il primo e migliore dei miei dialoghi: idea, titolo, trattamento, stile, tutto è stato deciso durante un pasto a 3 franchi e 50 centesimi. Delle cene sfrenate che facevo con te, non rimane nulla se non il ricordo di aver mangiato e bevuto troppo. E il fatto che io abbia ceduto alle tue richieste è stato dannoso per entrambi. Ora lo sai. Questo mi ha reso spesso avido, a volte addirittura senza scrupoli. E sempre scortese. In troppe occasioni, essere il tuo ospite non significava altro che gioia o privilegi. Hai dimenticato. Non parlo della formalità delle ringraziamenti, perché queste cortesie formali possono danneggiare una vera amicizia. Ma semplicemente della gentilezza nella compagnia reciproca, del piacere di conversare. Di tutte quelle cose che rendono la vita più bella, come la musica che accompagna le nostre giornate, mantenendo l’armonia e aggiungendo melodia ai momenti duri o silenziosi. E anche se può sembrarti strano che una persona nella mia terribile situazione possa distinguere tra una vergogna e un’altra, ammetto francamente che il fatto di aver sprecato tutto quel denaro per te, permettendoti di dissipare la mia fortuna a tuo vantaggio, ma anche al mio svantaggio, mi fa sentire parte di una comune follia. Una follia che mi rende doppiamente vergognoso del mio fallimento. Io sono fatto per qualcos’altro.
Ma soprattutto mi biasimo per tutta quella degradazione etica che ho permesso che tu mi infliggessi. La base del carattere è la forza di volontà, e la mia forza di volontà è diventata completamente soggetta alla tua. Sembra una cosa [[concepts/grotesque|grottesca]] da dire, ma è pur sempre vera. Quegli atti incessanti che per te sembravano quasi indispensabili, in cui la tua mente e il tuo [[concepts/body|-corpo]] venivano distorti, trasformandoti in qualcosa di orribile sia a vedersi che ad ascoltarsi: quella terribile mania che hai ereditato da tuo padre, quella ossessione per scrivere lettere rivoltanti e disgustose; la tua totale incapacità di controllare le emozioni, evidente nei tuoi lunghi periodi di risentimento e silenzio cupo, così come nelle improvvisse crisi di rabbia quasi epilettica. Tutti questi fattori hanno portato al mio fatale cedimento alle tue richieste sempre più insistenti. Tu hai vinto; è stato il trionfo del più debole sul più forte. È esattamente quel tipo di tirannia del debole sul forte che, in uno dei miei drammi, descrivo come “l’unica tirannia che dura”.
Era inevitabile. In ogni relazione con gli altri, è necessario trovare un modo per vivere. Nel tuo caso, si poteva o cedere a te o rinunciare a te. Non esisteva altra alternativa. A causa di un affetto profondo, anche se mal riposto verso di te; a causa della grande compassione per i tuoi difetti di carattere e temperamento; a causa della mia stessa natura gentile e della mia indolenza celtica; a causa di una repulsione artistica per scene volgari e parole brutte; a causa di quella incapacità di provare rancore di qualsiasi tipo che, in quel momento, caratterizzava me. Per tutte queste ragioni, semplici quanto possano sembrare, ho sempre ceduto a te. Di conseguenza, le tue richieste, i tuoi tentativi di dominio, le tue pretese sono diventate sempre più irragionevoli. Il tuo movente più meschino, il tuo desiderio più basso, la tua passione più comune sono diventati per te leggi secondo cui le vite degli altri dovevano essere guidate, e, se necessario, sacrificare senza esitazione. Sapendo che facendo scenate potevi sempre ottenere ciò che volevi, era naturale che tu procedessi, quasi inconsciamente, verso ogni forma di violenza volgare. Alla fine, non sapevi nemmeno a quale scopo stavi correndo. Avevi sfruttato il mio genio, la mia forza di volontà e la mia fortuna. E, nella cecità di una sete inesauribile, volevi tutta la mia esistenza. L’hai presa. Nel momento più critico della mia vita, proprio prima che compissi quell’azione assurda, da un lato c’era tuo padre che mi attaccava con lettere orribili; dall’altro, tu che mi attaccavi con lettere altrettanto disgustose. La lettera che hai ricevuto quella mattina, quando ti ho lasciato portarmi al tribunale di polizia per chiedere quell’ordine assurdo di arresto di tuo padre, era una delle peggiori che avessi mai scritto, e per il motivo più vergognoso. Tra voi due, ho perso la testa. La mia capacità di giudizio mi ha abbandonato. Il terrore ha preso il sopravvento. Non vedevo alcuna via d’uscita, davvero. Davanti a voi due, barcollavo come un bue nel caos. Avevo commesso un errore psicologico enorme. Pensavo sempre che cedere a te in piccole cose non significasse nulla. Che, quando sarebbe arrivato il momento decisivo, avrei potuto riaffermare la mia forza di volontà. Ma non era così. Nel momento cruciale, la mia forza di volontà mi ha completamente abbandonato. Nella vita, in realtà, non esistono cose piccole o grandi. Tutto ha lo stesso valore. Il mio abitudine – nata soprattutto dall’indifferenza all’inizio – di cedere a te in tutto è diventata, senza che me ne rendessi conto, una parte integrante della mia natura. Senza che io lo sapessi, aveva plasmato il mio carattere in uno stato d’animo permanente e fatale.
Ecco perché, nell’epilogo sottile della prima edizione dei suoi saggi, Pater afferma che “Il fallimento consiste nel formare abitudini”. Quando lo disse, le persone noiose di [[settings/oxford|Oxford]] considerarono quella frase semplicemente una capricciosa inversione del testo un po’ noioso dell’Etica aristotelica, ma in essa si nasconde una verità meravigliosa, e terribile. Ho permesso che tu minassero la mia forza di carattere, e per me la formazione di abitudini non è stata altro che rovina. Dal punto di vista etico, hai avuto su di me un effetto ancora più distruttivo rispetto a quello artistico.
Una volta concesso l’ordine di arresto, ovviamente il tuo testamento regolava tutto. In un momento in cui avrei dovuto trovarmi a [[settings/london|Londra]] per prendere saggi consigli e riflettere con calma sulla terribile trappola nella quale mi ero lasciato intrappolare – quella che tuo padre chiama ancora oggi “una trappola mortale” – tu hai insistito affinché ti portassi a Monte Carlo, tra tutti i luoghi più disgustosi della terra. Perché tu potessi giocare d’azzardo tutto il giorno e tutta la [[motifs/night|notte]], finché il casinò fosse aperto. Quanto a me. Poiché il baccarat non mi attirava affatto, sono stato lasciato da solo all’esterno. Hai rifiutato persino di discuterne per cinque minuti della situazione in cui tu e tuo padre mi avevate messo. Il mio compito era soltanto pagare le tue spese d’albergo e coprire le tue perdite. Qualsiasi accenno alle difficoltà che mi aspettavano veniva considerato noioso. Una nuova marca di champagne ci era stata consigliata. Ma a te interessava molto di più quella.  
Al nostro ritorno a [[settings/london|Londra]], alcuni dei miei amici, che davvero desideravano il mio bene, mi hanno supplicato di andare all’estero e di non affrontare una prova impossibile. Tu hai attribuito loro motivi meschini per darmi quel consiglio, e codardia a me per ascoltarlo. Mi hai costretto a restare, per cercare di superare tutto questo con falsi testimoniaggi assurdi e stupidi. Alla fine sono stato arrestato. E tuo padre è diventato il “eroe” del momento. Anzi, molto di più di un semplice eroe. La tua famiglia, in modo strano, ora appartiene agli “immortali”. Perché, con quell’effetto grottesco che sembra quasi un elemento gotico nella storia, e che rende Clio la meno seria delle Muse, tuo padre vivrà per sempre tra quei genitori puri e onesti della letteratura scolastica domenicale. Il tuo posto è accanto al piccolo Samuele. Mentre io mi trovo nel più profondo abisso del Malebolge, tra Gilles de Rais e il Marchese di Sade.
Certo che avrei dovuto sbarazzarmi di te, avrei dovuto scacciarti dalla mia vita, proprio come un uomo si scuote di dosso ciò che lo ha pungito. Nella sua opera più meravigliosa, Eschilo ci parla del grande Signore che alleva nella sua casa il cucciolo di leone, quel piccolo leoncino, e lo ama perché accorre subito al suo richiamo e si umilia davanti a lui in cerca del cibo: “phaidropos poti cheira sainon te gastros anagkais phaidropos poti cheira, ”. Ma poi quel cucciolo cresce e rivela la natura della sua razza, distrugge il Signore, la sua casa e tutto ciò che possiede. Penso di essere stato proprio come lui. Ma il mio errore non è stato quello di essermi allontanato da te, bensì di averlo fatto troppo spesso. Per quel che ne so, ogni tre mesi interrompevo regolarmente la nostra “amicizia”, e ogni volta tu riuscivi, con suppliche, telegrammi, lettere, l’intervento dei tuoi amici e dei miei, a convincermi a riaccettarti. Quando, alla fine di marzo del ’93, lasciasti la mia casa a Torquay, decisi che non avrei mai più parlato con te, né ti avrei permesso di stare con me. Quella sera, prima della tua partenza, il comportamento che hai tenuto era stato davvero disgustoso. Scrivesti e telegrafasti da Bristol chiedendomi di perdonarti e di incontrarti. Il tuo tutore, che era rimasto, mi disse che a volte eri davvero irresponsabile nelle tue azioni e parole, e che la maggior parte degli studenti del Magdalen condivideva questa opinione. Accettai di incontrarti. E naturalmente ti perdonai. Lungo il tragitto verso la città, mi supplicasti di portarti al Savoy. Quell’incontro fu davvero fatale per me.
Tre mesi dopo, a giugno, ci trovavamo a Goring. Alcuni dei tuoi amici di [[settings/oxford|Oxford]] vennero a trovarci da sabato a lunedì. La mattina in cui se ne andarono, fai un putiferio così terribile e angosciante che ti dissi che dovevamo separarci. Ricordo molto bene: eravamo seduti sul campo da croquet, circondati dal bel prato, e io ti spiegavo che stavamo rovinandoci a vicenda la vita; che tu stavi distruggendo completamente la mia, e che ovviamente non riuscivo davvero a renderti felice. Che quindi una separazione irreversibile fosse l’unica cosa saggia da fare. Dopo pranzo te ne andasti in silenzio, lasciando indietro una delle tue lettere più offensive, chiedendo al maggiordomo di consegnarmela dopo la tua partenza. Prima ancora che trascorressero tre giorni, mi inviesti un telegramma da [[settings/london|Londra]] chiedendomi scusa e pregandomi di permetterti di tornare. Avevo fatto tutto questo per compiacerti: avevo assunto i tuoi stessi domestici su tua richiesta. Ero sempre profondamente dispiaciuta per quel terribile carattere di cui eri davvero vittima. Ti amavo, quindi ti lasciai tornare e ti perdonai. Tre mesi dopo ancora, a settembre, si verificarono nuovi episodi: il motivo fu che ti feci notare gli errori commessi nella tua tentativa di tradurre [[forms/translation|Salomé]]. A quel punto dovevi essere ormai un buon studioso di francese. Avresti dovuto sapere che quella traduzione non era degna né di te, né di uno studente ordinario di Oxford, né dell’opera che cercava di rappresentare. Ovviamente all’epoca non lo sapevi. In una delle tue lettere violente scrivesti che non avevi “nessun obbligo intellettuale nei miei confronti”. Ricordo che quando lessi quelle parole, pensai che fossero le uniche verità che mi avessi mai detto nel corso della nostra [[concepts/friendship|amicizia]]. Mi resi conto che una natura meno coltivata ti sarebbe probabilmente adatta molto di più. Non lo dico con amarezza, ma semplicemente come un fatto legato alla nostra convivenza. Alla fine, il fondamento di qualsiasi relazione, sia nel [[motifs/marriage|matrimonio]] che nell’[[concepts/friendship|amicizia]], è la conversazione. E una conversazione deve avere un terreno comune. Tra due persone con culture molto diverse, l’unico terreno possibile è il livello più basso.
